159	Four attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\rho{\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mechanism of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\eta_{\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

[Director 指令] **R2-W2 补充：Alt Diversity Measures appendix section**

数据已就绪：`docs/paper/data/alt_diversity_measures.txt`

新增 Appendix 一段 "Alternative Diversity Measures"（app:alt_diversity），内容：

比较 K_eff 与三种经典 diversity measures 的预测力（OLS FDR-corrected, 12 conditions pooled across K）：
- Q-statistic (Kuncheva & Whitaker, 2003): 2/12 sig, mean |β_std|=0.080, ρ=0.674 with K_eff
- Disagreement rate: 8/12 (7+/1−), mean |β_std|=0.186, ρ=0.861
- κ-diversity: 7/12 (7+/0−), mean |β_std|=0.194, ρ=0.942

结论句：K_eff 与 κ-diversity 近等价（ρ=0.94），Q-statistic 解释力最弱。K_eff 的优势在于其信息论解释（有效独立通道数）和与 panel 聚合理论的直接对应。

同时确认 verbosity MMLU ρ 已改回 0.91。

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}}
4	\label{app:proof}
5	
6	The replaced judge $J_K'$ is \emph{pivotal} exactly when $t{-}1$ of the remaining $K{-}1$ judges have already failed. Formally, let $S = \sum_{j=1}^{K-1} X_j^a$ denote the number of failures among the shared judges. The panel fails if and only if the $K$-th judge also fails when $S = t{-}1$, or when $S \geq t$ regardless of the $K$-th judge:
7	\begin{align}
8	\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) &= \Pr(S \geq t) + \Pr(S = t{-}1) \cdot \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid S = t{-}1).
9	\end{align}
10	Since $\Pr(X_{K'}^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K}) \geq \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K})$ for all attacks $a$ and realizations $\mathbf{x}_{-K}$ (by hypothesis), the second term is weakly larger for $\mathcal{P}'$ than for $\mathcal{P}$ under every attack. Taking the supremum over attacks preserves the inequality: $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}') \geq \mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$. The result holds under arbitrary inter-judge correlations and any attack space $\mathcal{A}$.
11	
12	\section{Robustness Correction Methods}
13	\label{app:robustness_methods}
14	
15	The 455 panels are constructed combinatorially from 15 shared judges: each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ panels, inducing correlated residuals across panel-level observations. Standard OLS assumes independent errors, potentially inflating significance. We apply three corrections.
16	
17	\paragraph{Clustered Standard Errors.} Because each panel of $K{=}3$ judges does not map to a single cluster, we perform three separate one-way clusterings (one for each judge position in the ordered panel tuple) using CR1 bias-corrected sandwich estimators (13 clusters per position). The reported standard error for each coefficient is the maximum across the three clusterings, yielding the most conservative inference.
18	
19	\paragraph{Mixed Effects.} For each of the three judge positions, we fit a linear mixed-effects model with a random intercept for the judge occupying that position, estimating $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ as fixed effects. The reported $p$-value for each coefficient is the maximum across the three models.
20	
21	\paragraph{Wild Cluster Bootstrap.} We perform $B{=}9{,}999$ bootstrap resamples using Rademacher weights, clustering by each of the three panel positions in turn and taking the maximum $p$-value across positions (the most conservative aggregation). This approach avoids distributional assumptions on the cluster structure.
22	
23	\section{Extended Analysis}
24	\label{app:extended_analysis}
25	
26	\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}
27	\label{app:bft_reversal}
28	
29	Under standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\eta_{\max}$ significant in 9 of 12 conditions and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in 7. Non-independence correction reveals an asymmetry: $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 5 to 8 of 12 conditions depending on correction method (clustered SE: 8/12; mixed effects: 7/12; wild cluster bootstrap: 5/12), while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses to 0--1/12 (clustered SE 0/12; bootstrap 1/12; mixed effects 3/12). The directional pattern (point estimates predominantly positive) persists, but statistical confidence is limited. The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship that becomes artifactual under adaptive attack (0--1/12 after correction).
30	
31	Under OLS, the sole negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient is ARC $\times$ sycophancy ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$, FDR $p = 0.013$), though this effect does not survive non-independence correction (clustered $p = 0.441$). The GAM taxonomy subsumes this individual exception: the directional pattern across all 6 shared-bias conditions is the stronger evidence, not any single condition's significance.
32	
33	\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}
34	\label{app:diversity_capability}
35	
36	The $r = 0.70$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
37	
38	Family-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \citep{kuncheva2003measures}.
39	
40	\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}
41	\label{app:aggregation_details}
42	
43	Bayesian calibration is associated with a shift in the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient from predominantly positive under majority vote to predominantly negative (6 of 7 significant coefficients negative). The mechanism is calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, effectively implementing robustness screening within the aggregation rule itself. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors almost perfectly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.89$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \citep{tambe2011security}.
44	
45	\begin{figure}[h]
46	\centering
47	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}
48	\caption{Distribution of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients across aggregation methods. Bayesian aggregation is associated with a sign change in $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (1 positive, 6 negative), contrasting sharply with majority voting (7 positive, 1 negative).}
49	\label{fig:aggregation}
50	\end{figure}
51	
52	\subsection{Aggregation at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}5$}{K=5}}
53	\label{app:aggregation_k5}
54	
55	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k5} extends the aggregation analysis to $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels) under wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$. Bayesian calibration neutralizes both predictors ($\eta_{\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12, $\bar{R}^2{=}0.081$), contrasting with majority vote where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (7/12). Threshold voting retains high predictability ($\bar{R}^2{=}0.506$) with both predictors significant (6/12 and 10/12). The Bayesian neutralization at $K{=}5$ extends the reversal observed at $K{=}3$ (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}): calibrated confusion matrices effectively remove both individual weakness and structural diversity as predictive channels.
56	
57	\begin{table}[h]
58	\centering
59	\small
60	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
61	\toprule
62	\textbf{Aggregation} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{\bar{R}^2}$ \\
63	\midrule
64	Majority   & 0/12  & 7/12  & 0.256 \\
65	Weighted   & 1/12  & 9/12  & 0.249 \\
66	Threshold  & 6/12  & 10/12 & 0.506 \\
67	Bayesian   & 1/12  & 0/12  & 0.081 \\
68	\bottomrule
69	\end{tabular}
70	\caption{Aggregation method comparison at $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels, wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). Bayesian calibration neutralizes both predictors, while majority vote preserves the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regime transition. Compare Table~\ref{tab:aggregation} ($K{=}3$).}
71	\label{tab:aggregation_k5}
72	\end{table}
73	
74	\subsection{Metric Robustness Under Partial Panel Information}
75	\label{app:oracle}
76	
77	The main analysis computes $\eta_{\max}$ with full knowledge of panel composition (all three judges known). In deployment, a practitioner selecting or auditing a panel may know only a subset of the constituent judges. We test whether $\eta_{\max}$ retains predictive power under such partial information by recomputing it from two of the three panel members: for each panel, we cycle through all $\binom{3}{1} = 3$ leave-one-out masks, compute $\eta_{\max}$ from the two known judges, and average the regression estimates across masking scenarios.
78	
79	Under the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\eta_{\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (8/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\eta_{\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\eta_{\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.
80	
81	\section{Panel Selection Strategy Comparison}
82	\label{app:panel_selection}
83	
84	\begin{figure}[h]
85	\centering
86	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}
87	\caption{Panel attack success rate under three selection strategies. $\min(\eta_{\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming that robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}
88	\label{fig:panel_design}
89	\end{figure}
90	
91	Figure~\ref{fig:panel_design} compares three panel selection strategies: $\min(\eta_{\max})$ (select the panel minimizing weakest-judge vulnerability), random selection, and $\max(K_{\mathrm{eff}})$ (maximize diversity). The $\min(\eta_{\max})$ strategy achieves the lowest ASR in all 12 conditions, consistent with the weakest-link dominance documented in \S\ref{sec:dominance}.
92	
93	\section{Forest Plot: Coefficient Estimates with Clustered Standard Errors}
94	\label{app:forest_plot}
95	
96	\begin{figure}[h]

98	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_forest_plot.pdf}
99	\caption{Standardized coefficients with clustered SE 95\% CIs for $\eta_{\max}$ (left) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (right) across 12 conditions. Filled circles: FDR $< 0.05$. $\eta_{\max}$ shows consistent positive effects (8/12 significant); $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ CIs predominantly contain zero (0/12 significant).}
100	\label{fig:forest_plot}
101	\end{figure}
102	
103	\section{Robustness Checks: Subsampling and Specification}
104	\label{app:robustness_checks}
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks} summarizes regression results across model subsets and alternative specifications at $K{=}5$, testing whether the regime transition is robust to collinearity reduction and specification choice.
107	
108	\begin{table}[h]
109	\centering
110	\small
111	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
112	\toprule
113	\textbf{Subset / Method} & $\boldsymbol{r}$ & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Trans.} \\
114	\midrule
115	Full bootstrap       & 0.70 & 0/12 & 7/12 & \checkmark \\
116	API-only (9) clust.  & 0.85 & 8/12 & 3/12 & --- \\
117	Cap-matched A (5A+4L) & 0.50 & ---  & 9/12 & \checkmark\checkmark \\
118	Cap-matched B (6A+3L) & 0.73 & ---  & 2/12 & --- \\
119	Fractional logit     & 0.70 & 5/12 & 9/12 & \checkmark \\
120	\bottomrule
121	\end{tabular}
122	\caption{Regime transition robustness at $K{=}5$ across model subsets and specifications. $r$: correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$. $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ columns show FDR-significant counts out of 12 conditions. Trans.: whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates $\eta_{\max}$. API-only models ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) show partially collinear predictors; capability-matched subsamples ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen the transition. Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} confirms OLS patterns with 93.1\% directional agreement.}
123	\label{tab:robustness_checks}
124	\end{table}
125	
126	The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) demonstrates that elevated collinearity between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ partially inflates standard errors: both predictors achieve moderate significance but neither dominates, and the transition does not replicate. Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.50$) strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the transition weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 3--4 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
127	
128	\section{Random Subsample Analysis}
129	\label{app:random_subsample}
130	
131	To test whether the regime transition depends on model pool composition, we randomly sample 9 of 15 models (1{,}000 trials), construct all $\binom{9}{5}$ panels, and run the bootstrap analysis at $K{=}5$. We stratify by the number of local models ($n_{\mathrm{local}} \in \{0, \ldots, 6\}$) in each subsample.
132	
133	Under clustered standard errors, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the dominant predictor in 51.4\% of subsamples (mean significance 1.85/12), with an inverted-U as a function of $n_{\mathrm{local}}$: subsamples with 3--4 local models (maximizing capability range) achieve the highest $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance rate, while all-API ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{=}0$) and heavily-local ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{\geq}5$) subsamples show reduced detection. Significance is attenuated relative to the full pool by smaller sample sizes (126 vs.\ 3{,}003 panels at $K{=}5$), but the transition direction holds in the majority of subsamples. This confirms that the transition's detectability depends on capability diversity in the model pool rather than pool size per se.
134	
135	\section{Attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability}
136	\label{app:attacked_keff}
137	
138	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean (unattacked) pairwise MI (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), but used to predict vulnerability under attack. We verify this assumption by recomputing MI from judge outputs under each attack type and comparing clean vs.\ attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ via Spearman rank correlation across all panels.
139	
140	\begin{table}[h]
141	\centering
142	\small
143	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
144	\toprule
145	\textbf{Attack} & \textbf{MMLU $\rho$} & \textbf{ARC $\rho$} \\
146	\midrule
147	Score manipulation & 0.98 & 0.99 \\
148	Authority bias     & 0.95 & 0.97 \\
149	Sycophancy         & 0.91 & 0.92 \\
150	Verbosity bias     & 0.91 & 0.92 \\
151	Prompt injection   & 0.63 & 0.60 \\
152	Position bias      & 0.60 & 0.08 \\
153	\bottomrule
154	\end{tabular}
155	\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\rho{\geq}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$); position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}
156	\label{tab:attacked_keff}
157	\end{table}
158	
159	Four attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\rho{\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mechanism of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\eta_{\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.
160	
161	\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}
162	\label{app:sensitivity}
163	
164	We apply the Cinelli--Hazlett omitted variable bias framework to quantify how robust $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is to unobserved confounders. For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \times 2$ benchmarks $\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have, in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome, to reduce $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.
165	
166	\begin{table}[h]
167	\centering
168	\footnotesize
169	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
170	\begin{tabular}{rcllcccc}
171	\toprule
172	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{Data} & \textbf{Attack} & \textbf{RV} & $\boldsymbol{\eta}$ $\boldsymbol{R^2_{\mathrm{tr}}}$ & $\boldsymbol{\eta}$ $\boldsymbol{R^2_{\mathrm{out}}}$ & \textbf{Exc.} \\
173	\midrule
174	3 & MMLU & Prompt inj.    & 0.419 & 0.587 & 0.059 & -- \\
175	3 & MMLU & Sycophancy     & 0.412 & 0.347 & 0.098 & \checkmark \\
176	3 & MMLU & Score manip.   & 0.410 & 0.735 & 0.002 & -- \\

RV: robustness value (minimum partial $R^2$ an unobserved confounder must have with both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and ASR to nullify the coefficient). $\eta$ $R^2_{\mathrm{tr}}$/$R^2_{\mathrm{out}}$: partial $R^2$ of $\eta_{\max}$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (treatment) and ASR (outcome). Exc.: \checkmark\ if RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$'s partial $R^2$ with treatment, indicating $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is robust to the known capability confound. 217	
218	RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across the 36 conditions (median by $K$: 0.30 at $K{=}3$, 0.22 at $K{=}5$, 0.21 at $K{=}7$), revealing heterogeneous robustness: some conditions show $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients robust to strong confounders (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU prompt injection, RV${=}0.52$), while others are fragile (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU authority bias, RV${=}0.005$). The known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 11/12, $K{=}5$: 9/12, $K{=}7$: 8/12). The fraction of conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to the $\eta_{\max}$ confound increases with panel size (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12), paralleling the regime transition documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_k}.

Contour lines show the adjusted $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under hypothetical confounders of varying strength. The diamond marks the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark; conditions where the benchmark falls inside the zero-contour indicate that the $\eta_{\max}$ confound alone could explain away $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect.}
224	\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}
225	\end{figure}
226	
227	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
228	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
229	
230	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.
231	
232	\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}
233	\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}
234	
235	To test whether reducing the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.

243	\midrule

245	Full / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\

RV $>$ $\eta$: conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\ $0.70$) doubles the fraction of robust conditions at both $K$ values. Conservative count at $K{=}5$: 5/12 (excluding MMLU $\times$ authority, $R^2{\approx}0$).}

253	
254	At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (5/12 conservatively), concentrated in ARC (5/6 exceeds) with limited MMLU improvement (1/6). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 6/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity partially separates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.
255	
256	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
257	\label{app:epsilon}
258	
259	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Despite this variation, standardized coefficient differences between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$ are $<$0.01, and qualitative conclusions (significance patterns, directional signs) are identical. FDR significance counts are stable across all three $\epsilon$ values. As an $\epsilon$-free robustness check, fractional logit regression \citep{papke1996econometric} achieves 93.1\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the regime transition pattern (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
260	
261	\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}
262	\label{app:power_analysis}
263	
264	Table~\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an informative null (true effect $<$0.14 SD); conversely, the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.

      "content": "\\appendix\n\n\\section{Proof of Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}}\n\\label{app:proof}\n\nThe replaced judge $J_K'$ is \\emph{pivotal} exactly when $t{-}1$ of the remaining $K{-}1$ judges have already failed. Formally, let $S = \\sum_{j=1}^{K-1} X_j^a$ denote the number of failures among the shared judges. The panel fails if and only if the $K$-th judge also fails when $S = t{-}1$, or when $S \\geq t$ regardless of the $K$-th judge:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) &= \\Pr(S \\geq t) + \\Pr(S = t{-}1) \\cdot \\Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \\mid S = t{-}1).\n\\end{align}\nSince $\\Pr(X_{K'}^a {=} 1 \\mid \\mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \\mathbf{x}_{-K}) \\geq \\Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \\mid \\mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \\mathbf{x}_{-K})$ for all attacks $a$ and realizations $\\mathbf{x}_{-K}$ (by hypothesis), the second term is weakly larger for $\\mathcal{P}'$ than for $\\mathcal{P}$ under every attack. Taking the supremum over attacks preserves the inequality: $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}') \\geq \\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$. The result holds under arbitrary inter-judge correlations and any attack space $\\mathcal{A}$.\n\n\\section{Robustness Correction Methods}\n\\label{app:robustness_methods}\n\nThe 455 panels are constructed combinatorially from 15 shared judges: each judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ panels, inducing correlated residuals across panel-level observations. Standard OLS assumes independent errors, potentially inflating significance. We apply three corrections.\n\n\\paragraph{Clustered Standard Errors.} Because each panel of $K{=}3$ judges does not map to a single cluster, we perform three separate one-way clusterings (one for each judge position in the ordered panel tuple) using CR1 bias-corrected sandwich estimators (13 clusters per position). The reported standard error for each coefficient is the maximum across the three clusterings, yielding the most conservative inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Mixed Effects.} For each of the three judge positions, we fit a linear mixed-effects model with a random intercept for the judge occupying that position, estimating $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ as fixed effects. The reported $p$-value for each coefficient is the maximum across the three models.\n\n\\paragraph{Wild Cluster Bootstrap.} We perform $B{=}9{,}999$ bootstrap resamples using Rademacher weights, clustering by each of the three panel positions in turn and taking the maximum $p$-value across positions (the most conservative aggregation). This approach avoids distributional assumptions on the cluster structure.\n\n\\section{Extended Analysis}\n\\label{app:extended_analysis}\n\n\\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}\n\\label{app:bft_reversal}\n\nUnder standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\\eta_{\\max}$ significant in 9 of 12 conditions and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in 7. Non-independence correction reveals an asymmetry: $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 5 to 8 of 12 conditions depending on correction method (clustered SE: 8/12; mixed effects: 7/12; wild cluster bootstrap: 5/12), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses to 0--1/12 (clustered SE 0/12; bootstrap 1/12; mixed effects 3/12). The directional pattern (point estimates predominantly positive) persists, but statistical confidence is limited. The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship that becomes artifactual under adaptive attack (0--1/12 after correction).\n\nUnder OLS, the sole negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient is ARC $\\times$ sycophancy ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$, FDR $p = 0.013$), though this effect does not survive non-independence correction (clustered $p = 0.441$). The GAM taxonomy subsumes this individual exception: the directional pattern across all 6 shared-bias conditions is the stronger evidence, not any single condition's significance.\n\n\\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}\n\\label{app:diversity_capability}\n\nThe $r = 0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.\n\nFamily-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}\n\\label{app:aggregation_details}\n\nBayesian calibration is associated with a shift in the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient from predominantly positive under majority vote to predominantly negative (6 of 7 significant coefficients negative). The mechanism is calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, effectively implementing robustness screening within the aggregation rule itself. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors almost perfectly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.89$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \\citep{tambe2011security}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Distribution of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients across aggregation methods. Bayesian aggregation is associated with a sign change in $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (1 positive, 6 negative), contrasting sharply with majority voting (7 positive, 1 negative).}\n\\label{fig:aggregation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}5$}{K=5}}\n\\label{app:aggregation_k5}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5} extends the aggregation analysis to $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels) under wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$. Bayesian calibration neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12, $\\bar{R}^2{=}0.081$), contrasting with majority vote where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (7/12). Threshold voting retains high predictability ($\\bar{R}^2{=}0.506$) with both predictors significant (6/12 and 10/12). The Bayesian neutralization at $K{=}5$ extends the reversal observed at $K{=}3$ (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}): calibrated confusion matrices effectively remove both individual weakness and structural diversity as predictive channels.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{\\bar{R}^2}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority   & 0/12  & 7/12  & 0.256 \\\\\nWeighted   & 1/12  & 9/12  & 0.249 \\\\\nThreshold  & 6/12  & 10/12 & 0.506 \\\\\nBayesian   & 1/12  & 0/12  & 0.081 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison at $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels, wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Bayesian calibration neutralizes both predictors, while majority vote preserves the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regime transition. Compare Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation} ($K{=}3$).}\n\\label{tab:aggregation_k5}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Metric Robustness Under Partial Panel Information}\n\\label{app:oracle}\n\nThe main analysis computes $\\eta_{\\max}$ with full knowledge of panel composition (all three judges known). In deployment, a practitioner selecting or auditing a panel may know only a subset of the constituent judges. We test whether $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains predictive power under such partial information by recomputing it from two of the three panel members: for each panel, we cycle through all $\\binom{3}{1} = 3$ leave-one-out masks, compute $\\eta_{\\max}$ from the two known judges, and average the regression estimates across masking scenarios.\n\nUnder the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\\eta_{\\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (8/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\\eta_{\\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\\eta_{\\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.\n\n\\section{Panel Selection Strategy Comparison}\n\\label{app:panel_selection}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel attack success rate under three selection strategies. $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming that robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}\n\\label{fig:panel_design}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:panel_design} compares three panel selection strategies: $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ (select the panel minimizing weakest-judge vulnerability), random selection, and $\\max(K_{\\mathrm{eff}})$ (maximize diversity). The $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ strategy achieves the lowest ASR in all 12 conditions, consistent with the weakest-link dominance documented in \\S\\ref{sec:dominance}.\n\n\\section{Forest Plot: Coefficient Estimates with Clustered Standard Errors}\n\\label{app:forest_plot}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Standardized coefficients with clustered SE 95\\% CIs for $\\eta_{\\max}$ (left) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (right) across 12 conditions. Filled circles: FDR $< 0.05$. $\\eta_{\\max}$ shows consistent positive effects (8/12 significant); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ CIs predominantly contain zero (0/12 significant).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Robustness Checks: Subsampling and Specification}\n\\label{app:robustness_checks}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:robustness_checks} summarizes regression results across model subsets and alternative specifications at $K{=}5$, testing whether the regime transition is robust to collinearity reduction and specification choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Subset / Method} & $\\boldsymbol{r}$ & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Trans.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull bootstrap       & 0.70 & 0/12 & 7/12 & \\checkmark \\\\\nAPI-only (9) clust.  & 0.85 & 8/12 & 3/12 & --- \\\\\nCap-matched A (5A+4L) & 0.50 & ---  & 9/12 & \\checkmark\\checkmark \\\\\nCap-matched B (6A+3L) & 0.73 & ---  & 2/12 & --- \\\\\nFractional logit     & 0.70 & 5/12 & 9/12 & \\checkmark \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Regime transition robustness at $K{=}5$ across model subsets and specifications. $r$: correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$. $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ columns show FDR-significant counts out of 12 conditions. Trans.: whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates $\\eta_{\\max}$. API-only models ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) show partially collinear predictors; capability-matched subsamples ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen the transition. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} confirms OLS patterns with 93.1\\% directional agreement.}\n\\label{tab:robustness_checks}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) demonstrates that elevated collinearity between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ partially inflates standard errors: both predictors achieve moderate significance but neither dominates, and the transition does not replicate. Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.50$) strengthens $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the transition weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 3--4 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\section{Random Subsample Analysis}\n\\label{app:random_subsample}\n\nTo test whether the regime transition depends on model pool composition, we randomly sample 9 of 15 models (1{,}000 trials), construct all $\\binom{9}{5}$ panels, and run the bootstrap analysis at $K{=}5$. We stratify by the number of local models ($n_{\\mathrm{local}} \\in \\{0, \\ldots, 6\\}$) in each subsample.\n\nUnder clustered standard errors, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is the dominant predictor in 51.4\\% of subsamples (mean significance 1.85/12), with an inverted-U as a function of $n_{\\mathrm{local}}$: subsamples with 3--4 local models (maximizing capability range) achieve the highest $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance rate, while all-API ($n_{\\mathrm{local}}{=}0$) and heavily-local ($n_{\\mathrm{local}}{\\geq}5$) subsamples show reduced detection. Significance is attenuated relative to the full pool by smaller sample sizes (126 vs.\\ 3{,}003 panels at $K{=}5$), but the transition direction holds in the majority of subsamples. This confirms that the transition's detectability depends on capability diversity in the model pool rather than pool size per se.\n\n\\section{Attacked $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability}\n\\label{app:attacked_keff}\n\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean (unattacked) pairwise MI (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), but used to predict vulnerability under attack. We verify this assumption by recomputing MI from judge outputs under each attack type and comparing clean vs.\\ attacked $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ via Spearman rank correlation across all panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{MMLU $\\rho$} & \\textbf{ARC $\\rho$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nScore manipulation & 0.98 & 0.99 \\\\\nAuthority bias     & 0.95 & 0.97 \\\\\nSycophancy         & 0.91 & 0.92 \\\\\nVerbosity bias     & 0.91 & 0.92 \\\\\nPrompt injection   & 0.63 & 0.60 \\\\\nPosition bias      & 0.60 & 0.08 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\\rho{\\approx}0.60$--$0.63$); position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}\n\\label{tab:attacked_keff}\n\\end{table}\n\nFour attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\\rho{\\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mechanism of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\\eta_{\\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.\n\n\\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:sensitivity}\n\nWe apply the Cinelli--Hazlett omitted variable bias framework to quantify how robust $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is to unobserved confounders. For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \\times 2$ benchmarks $\\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have, in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome, to reduce $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{rcllcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Data} & \\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{RV} & -- \\\\\n7 & ARC  & Authority bias & 0.179 & 0.514 & 0.003 & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across 36 conditions. RV: robustness value (minimum partial $R^2$ an unobserved confounder must have with both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and ASR to nullify the coefficient). $\\eta$ $R^2_{\\mathrm{tr}}$/$R^2_{\\mathrm{out}}$: partial $R^2$ of $\\eta_{\\max}$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (treatment) and ASR (outcome). Exc.: \\checkmark\\ if RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$'s partial $R^2$ with treatment, indicating $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is robust to the known capability confound. RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 8/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 1/12, $K{=}5$: 3/12, $K{=}7$: 4/12).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\nRV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across the 36 conditions (median by $K$: 0.30 at $K{=}3$, 0.22 at $K{=}5$, 0.21 at $K{=}7$), revealing heterogeneous robustness: some conditions show $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients robust to strong confounders (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU prompt injection, RV${=}0.52$), while others are fragile (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU authority bias, RV${=}0.005$). The known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 11/12, $K{=}5$: 9/12, $K{=}7$: 8/12). The fraction of conditions where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound increases with panel size (1/12 $\\to$ 3/12 $\\to$ 4/12), paralleling the regime transition documented in \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K5.png}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plot at $K{=}5$ (representative). $x$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with treatment ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$); $y$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with outcome (ASR). Contour lines show the adjusted $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under hypothetical confounders of varying strength. The diamond marks the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound benchmark; conditions where the benchmark falls inside the zero-contour indicate that the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound alone could explain away $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect.}\n\\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}\n\\label{app:cross_benchmark}\n\nTo test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.\n\n\\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}\n\nTo test whether reducing the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Pool / $K$} & \\textbf{RV $>$ $\\eta$} & \\textbf{Median RV} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull / $K{=}3$ & 1/12 & 0.30 & 1/12 \\\\\nFull / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\\\\nPool A / $K{=}3$ & 3/12 & 0.18 & --- \\\\\nPool A / $K{=}5$ & 6/12 & 0.22 & 9/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\\eta$: conditions where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\\ $0.70$) doubles the fraction of robust conditions at both $K$ values. Conservative count at $K{=}5$: 5/12 (excluding MMLU $\\times$ authority, $R^2{\\approx}0$).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (5/12 conservatively), concentrated in ARC (5/6 exceeds) with limited MMLU improvement (1/6). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 6/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity partially separates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.\n\n\\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:epsilon}\n\nThe log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Despite this variation, standardized coefficient differences between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$ are $<$0.01, and qualitative conclusions (significance patterns, directional signs) are identical. FDR significance counts are stable across all three $\\epsilon$ values. As an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check, fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} achieves 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the regime transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n\n\\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{app:power_analysis}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \\to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an informative null (true effect $<$0.14 SD); conversely, the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & \\textbf{MDE ($\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}$)} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 455     & 0.144 & 1/12 \\\\\n5 & 3{,}003 & 0.048 & 7/12 \\\\\n7 & 6{,}435 & 0.036 & 5/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Minimum detectable effect size (80\\% power, 1{,}000 simulations, wild cluster bootstrap) by panel size. MDE decreases approximately as $1/\\sqrt{N}$, confirming that $K{=}5$/$K{=}7$ significance reflects genuine effects rather than inflated type~I error.}\n\\label{tab:mde}\n\\end{table}\n",

MDE decreases approximately as $1/\\sqrt{N}$, confirming that $K{=}5$/$K{=}7$ significance reflects genuine effects rather than inflated type~I error.}\n\\label{tab:mde}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Alternative Diversity Measures}\n\\label{app:alt_diversity}\n\nWe compare $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ against three classical ensemble diversity measures using the same OLS specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with FDR correction across 12 conditions pooled over $K$ values.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Measure} & \\textbf{Sig} & \\textbf{Dir.} & \\textbf{$|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|$} & $\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$ \\textbf{w/} $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n$Q$-statistic    & 2/12  & 1+/1$-$ & 0.080 & 0.674 \\\\\nDisagreement     & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$ & 0.186 & 0.861 \\\\\n$\\kappa$-diversity & 7/12 & 7+/0$-$ & 0.194 & 0.942 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (ref.) & 8/12 & 7+/1$-$ & 0.164 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Comparison of diversity measures as predictors of panel ASR (OLS, FDR-corrected, 12 conditions pooled across $K$). Sig: FDR-significant conditions. $|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|$: mean absolute standardized coefficient. $\\rho$: Pearson correlation with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$.}\n\\label{tab:alt_diversity}\n\\end{table}\n\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} are near-equivalent ($\\rho{=}0.94$), with comparable significance counts (8/12 vs.\\ 7/12) and effect sizes (0.164 vs.\\ 0.194). Disagreement rate achieves similar predictive power ($\\rho{=}0.86$, 8/12). The $Q$-statistic is the weakest predictor (2/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.080$), consistent with its known insensitivity to the type of error correlation that drives panel vulnerability \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s advantage over these alternatives is not predictive power but interpretability: it quantifies the number of effective independent channels in the panel, connecting directly to the information-theoretic foundation of panel aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

          " \\end{table}",

          "+\\section{Alternative Diversity Measures}",
          "+\\label{app:alt_diversity}",
          "+",

Sig: FDR-significant conditions. $|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|$: mean absolute standardized coefficient. $\\rho$: Pearson correlation with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$.}",

Disagreement rate achieves similar predictive power ($\\rho{=}0.86$, 8/12). The $Q$-statistic is the weakest predictor (2/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.080$), consistent with its known insensitivity to the type of error correlation that drives panel vulnerability \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s advantage over these alternatives is not predictive power but interpretability: it quantifies the number of effective independent channels in the panel, connecting directly to the information-theoretic foundation of panel aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments})."

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}
5	\label{sec:dominance}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\ref{tab:models}).
8	
9	\begin{table}[t]
10	\centering
11	\small
12	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
13	\toprule
14	\textbf{Bench.} & \textbf{Attack} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Dom.} \\
15	\midrule
16	MMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
17	MMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
18	MMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & n.s.    & -- \\
19	MMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
20	MMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
21	MMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
22	\addlinespace
23	ARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
24	ARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $-^{*}$ & Co \\
25	ARC  & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
26	ARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
27	ARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
28	ARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
29	\bottomrule
30	\end{tabular}
31	\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\ column: $\eta$ = $\eta_{\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \citep{budescu1993dominance}.}
32	\label{tab:dominance}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	$\eta_{\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation, ARC $\times$ score manipulation, and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 3 $\eta_{\max}$-dominant, 6 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 1 condition where neither predictor reaches significance. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.
36	
37	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Table~\ref{tab:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections.
38	
39	\begin{table}[t]
40	\centering
41	\small
42	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
43	\toprule
44	\textbf{Method} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{dir.} & $\boldsymbol{\eta}$\textbf{:}$\boldsymbol{K}$ \\
45	\midrule
46	OLS                   & 9/12  & 8/12 & 7+/1$-$ & 1.1:1 \\
47	Clustered SE (CR1)    & 8/12  & 0/12 & ---     & ---   \\
48	Mixed effects         & 7/12  & 3/12 & 3+/0$-$ & 2.3:1 \\
49	Wild cluster bootstrap & 5/12  & 1/12 & 1+/0$-$ & 5:1   \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}
52	\caption{Robustness of regression results to non-independence correction ($K{=}3$). Clustered SE uses 3-position max-SE with CR1 correction (13 clusters per position). Mixed effects uses 3-position max-$p$ with random judge intercepts. Wild cluster bootstrap uses $B{=}9999$ resamples, Rademacher weights, and max-$p$ across 3 panel positions. $\eta$:$K$ ratio = $\eta_{\max}$ significant conditions / $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant conditions.}
53	\label{tab:robustness}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	$\eta_{\max}$ remains robust: 8/12 under clustered SE, 7/12 under mixed effects, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap (Figure~\ref{fig:robustness}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses under correction: OLS's 8/12 reduces to 0/12 (clustered SE) and 1/12 (bootstrap). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
57	
58	\begin{figure}[t]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}
61	\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}
62	\label{fig:robustness}
63	\end{figure}
64	
65	Power analysis yields MDE $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD.
66	
67	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}
68	\label{sec:cross_k}
69	
70	The $K{=}3$ results above show that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ fails to reach significance under corrected inference. A natural question is whether this negligibility is intrinsic to judge diversity or an artifact of small panel size. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
71	
72	\begin{table}[t]
73	\centering
74	\small
75	\begin{tabular}{rrccc}
76	\toprule
77	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{Panels} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{dir.} \\
78	\midrule
79	3 & 455     & 5/12 & 1/12 & 1+/0$-$ \\
80	5 & 3{,}003 & 0/12 & 7/12 & 7+/0$-$ \\
81	7 & 6{,}435 & 0/12 & 5/12 & 5+/0$-$ \\
82	\bottomrule
83	\end{tabular}
84	\caption{Cross-$K$ wild cluster bootstrap summary ($B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, $K$-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$, $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$). As panel size increases, $\eta_{\max}$ significance drops from 5/12 to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises from 1/12 to 7/12. All significant $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients are positive.}
85	\label{tab:cross_k}
86	\end{table}
87	
88	Table~\ref{tab:cross_k} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
89	
90	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
91	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
92	
93	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256 but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (7/12). The gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.
94	
95	\begin{figure}[t]
96	\centering
97	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
98	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}
99	\label{fig:gam_split}
100	\end{figure}
101	
102	\subsection{Attack-Type Empirical Categorization}
103	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
104	
105	GAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions; \textit{shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6. Position bias is an anomalous case within the targeted category: while it shows positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction, its $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ stability is low ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}), placing it closer to a measurement-unstable regime than to the other targeted attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.60$). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.
106	
107	\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}
108	\label{sec:aggregation}
109	
110	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.
111	
112	\begin{table}[t]
113	\centering
114	\small
115	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
116	\toprule
117	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ $\beta$ dir.} & \textbf{$R^2$} \\
118	\midrule
119	Threshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\
120	Majority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\
121	Weighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\
122	Bayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\
123	\bottomrule
124	\end{tabular}
125	\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}
126	\label{tab:aggregation}
127	\end{table}
128	
129	
130	The methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. Bayesian calibration is associated with a sign change in the diversity channel: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction shifts from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).
131

Wild cluster bootstrap uses $B{=}9999$ resamples, Rademacher weights, and max-$p$ across 3 panel positions. $\\eta$:$K$ ratio = $\\eta_{\\max}$ significant conditions / $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant conditions.}\n\\label{tab:robustness}\n\\end{table}\n\n$\\eta_{\\max}$ remains robust: 8/12 under clustered SE, 7/12 under mixed effects, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap (Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses under correction: OLS's 8/12 reduces to 0/12 (clustered SE) and 1/12 (bootstrap). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \\emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD.\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ results above show that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ fails to reach significance under corrected inference. A natural question is whether this negligibility is intrinsic to judge diversity or an artifact of small panel size. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{dir.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 455     & 5/12 & 1/12 & 1+/0$-$ \\\\\n5 & 3{,}003 & 0/12 & 7/12 & 7+/0$-$ \\\\\n7 & 6{,}435 & 0/12 & 5/12 & 5+/0$-$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-$K$ wild cluster bootstrap summary ($B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, $K$-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$, $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$). As panel size increases, $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance drops from 5/12 to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises from 1/12 to 7/12. All significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients are positive.}\n\\label{tab:cross_k}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_k} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256 but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (7/12). The gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Empirical Categorization}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \\textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions; \\textit{shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6. Position bias is an anomalous case within the targeted category: while it shows positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction, its $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ stability is low ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}), placing it closer to a measurement-unstable regime than to the other targeted attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.60$). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\\\\nMajority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. Bayesian calibration is associated with a sign change in the diversity channel: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction shifts from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).\n",

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\eta_{\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\geq}3$ judges, and high-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.
6	
7	\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}
8	The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.
9	
10	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
11	The $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), with robustness increasing with $K$ (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12), paralleling the regime transition. The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12), raising the possibility that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ partly proxies for quantization-induced capability variance. Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12; Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), versus 3/12 in the full pool. Cross-benchmark prediction ($r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}) further supports structural rather than benchmark-specific origins. The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled designs.
12	
13	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
14	Bayesian calibration is associated with a reversal of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ (6/7 negative; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}) and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$ (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}). Recomputing MI under attack confirms stability ($\rho{\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks; position bias disrupts MI on ARC, $\rho{=}0.08$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}). The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) confirms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse most pronounced at $K{=}3$.
15	
```

19	\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}
20	
21	\author{Anonymous}
22	
23	\begin{document}
24	\maketitle
25	
26	\begin{abstract}
27	Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration corresponding to a sign change in the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence.
28	\end{abstract}
29	
30	\input{introduction}
31	\input{related_work}
32	\input{threat_model}
33	\input{methodology}
34	\input{results}
35	\input{analysis}
36	\input{conclusion}
37	
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$. We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, which may partly explain the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming); the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization (Table~\ref{tab:models}). Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\lceil K/2\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$), changing the threshold mechanism. The transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
41	
42	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
43	Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.
44
