      "content": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration corresponding to a sign change in the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$. We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, which may partly explain the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming); the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\\lceil K/2\\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$), changing the threshold mechanism. The transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
      "numLines": 51,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 51
    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NG6xUwc8QbEhRqF9RVKMYm`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We document a descriptive regime transition in LLM judge panel vulnerability: individual weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) dominates at $K{=}3$; at $K{\geq}5$, diversity structure ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes the primary predictor. The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ association is attack-mechanism-dependent (targeted positive, shared-bias negative); Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$. Because $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ are structurally correlated ($r{=}0.70$), the independent causal contribution of diversity remains only partially separable even under capability-matched subsampling (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). For deployment: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual robustness; at $K{\geq}5$, prefer correlated robustness profiles. Causal identification requires training-based diversity control \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}.
4	
```

15	\midrule

17	MMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
18	MMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & n.s.    & -- \\
19	MMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
20	MMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
21	MMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
22	\addlinespace
23	ARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
24	ARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $-^{*}$ & Co \\
25	ARC  & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
26	ARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
27	ARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
28	ARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
29	\bottomrule
30	\end{tabular}
31	\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\ column: $\eta$ = $\eta_{\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \citep{budescu1993dominance}.}
32	\label{tab:dominance}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	$\eta_{\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation, ARC $\times$ score manipulation, and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 3 $\eta_{\max}$-dominant, 6 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 1 condition where neither predictor reaches significance. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.
36	
37	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: $\eta_{\max}$ remains robust (8/12 under clustered SE, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap), while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses (0/12 and 1/12 respectively). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
38	
39	\begin{figure}[t]
40	\centering
41	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}
42	\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}
43	\label{fig:robustness}
44	\end{figure}
45	
46	Power analysis yields MDE $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD.
47	
48	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}
49	\label{sec:cross_k}
50	
51	The $K{=}3$ results above show that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ fails to reach significance under corrected inference. A natural question is whether this negligibility is intrinsic to judge diversity or an artifact of small panel size. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
52	
53	\begin{table}[t]
54	\centering
55	\small
56	\begin{tabular}{rrccc}
57	\toprule
58	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{Panels} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{dir.} \\
59	\midrule
60	3 & 455     & 5/12 & 1/12 & 1+/0$-$ \\
61	5 & 3{,}003 & 0/12 & 7/12 & 7+/0$-$ \\
62	7 & 6{,}435 & 0/12 & 5/12 & 5+/0$-$ \\
63	\bottomrule
64	\end{tabular}
65	\caption{Cross-$K$ wild cluster bootstrap summary ($B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, $K$-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$, $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$). As panel size increases, $\eta_{\max}$ significance drops from 5/12 to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises from 1/12 to 7/12. All significant $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients are positive.}
66	\label{tab:cross_k}
67	\end{table}
68	
69	Table~\ref{tab:cross_k} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
70	
71	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
72	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
73	
74	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256 but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (7/12). The gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.
75	
76	\begin{figure}[t]
77	\centering
78	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
79	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}
80	\label{fig:gam_split}
81	\end{figure}
82	
83	\subsection{Attack-Type Empirical Categorization}
84	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
85	
86	GAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions; \textit{shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6. Position bias is an anomalous case within the targeted category: while it shows positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction, its $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ stability is low ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}), placing it closer to a measurement-unstable regime than to the other targeted attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.60$). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.
87	
88	\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}
89	\label{sec:aggregation}
90	
91	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.
92	
93	\begin{table}[t]
94	\centering
95	\small
96	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
97	\toprule
98	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ $\beta$ dir.} & \textbf{$R^2$} \\
99	\midrule
100	Threshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\
101	Majority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\
102	Weighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\
103	Bayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\
104	\bottomrule
105	\end{tabular}
106	\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}
107	\label{tab:aggregation}
108	\end{table}
109	
110	
111	The methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. Bayesian calibration is associated with a sign change in the diversity channel: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction shifts from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).
112	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & n.s.    & -- \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $-^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\n$\\eta_{\\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation, ARC $\\times$ score manipulation, and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 3 $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant, 6 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 1 condition where neither predictor reaches significance. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: $\\eta_{\\max}$ remains robust (8/12 under clustered SE, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses (0/12 and 1/12 respectively). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \\emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD.\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ results above show that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ fails to reach significance under corrected inference. A natural question is whether this negligibility is intrinsic to judge diversity or an artifact of small panel size. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{dir.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 455     & 5/12 & 1/12 & 1+/0$-$ \\\\\n5 & 3{,}003 & 0/12 & 7/12 & 7+/0$-$ \\\\\n7 & 6{,}435 & 0/12 & 5/12 & 5+/0$-$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-$K$ wild cluster bootstrap summary ($B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, $K$-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$, $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$). As panel size increases, $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance drops from 5/12 to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises from 1/12 to 7/12. All significant $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients are positive.}\n\\label{tab:cross_k}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_k} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256 but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (7/12). The gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Empirical Categorization}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \\textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions; \\textit{shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6. Position bias is an anomalous case within the targeted category: while it shows positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction, its $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ stability is low ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}), placing it closer to a measurement-unstable regime than to the other targeted attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.60$). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\\\\nMajority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. Bayesian calibration is associated with a sign change in the diversity channel: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction shifts from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. Cross-benchmark prediction ($r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}) further supports structural rather than benchmark-specific origins.   "old_string": "Our study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$. We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, which may partly explain the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming); the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\\lceil K/2\\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$), changing the threshold mechanism. The transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
  "new_string": "Our study has three principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence; we address this with wild cluster bootstrap ($K$-position max-$p$), but the correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, partly explaining the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 ($K{=}5$) to 5/12 ($K{=}7$). Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface differs structurally from open-ended evaluation; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization. Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified, and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis})."
}

The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming); the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\\lceil K/2\\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$), changing the threshold mechanism. The transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
          "+Our study has three principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence; we address this with wild cluster bootstrap ($K$-position max-$p$), but the correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, partly explaining the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 ($K{=}5$) to 5/12 ($K{=}7$). Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface differs structurally from open-ended evaluation; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization. Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified, and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
          " ",
          " \\section*{Code and Data Availability}",
          " Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility."
        ]
      }
    ],

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UhCin8h3X2ZYgrQRrmB4n5`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We document a descriptive regime transition in LLM judge panel vulnerability: individual weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) dominates at $K{=}3$; at $K{\geq}5$, diversity structure ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes the primary predictor. The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ association is attack-mechanism-dependent (targeted positive, shared-bias negative); Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$. Because $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ are structurally correlated ($r{=}0.70$), the independent causal contribution of diversity remains only partially separable even under capability-matched subsampling (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). For deployment: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual robustness; at $K{\geq}5$, prefer correlated robustness profiles. Causal identification requires training-based diversity control \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}.
4	
```

Conservative count at $K{=}5$: 5/12 (excluding MMLU $\times$ authority, $R^2{\approx}0$).}
251	\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}
252	\end{table}
253	
254	At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (5/12 conservatively), concentrated in ARC (5/6 exceeds) with limited MMLU improvement (1/6). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 6/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity partially separates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.

```text
33	\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}
34	\label{app:diversity_capability}
35	
36	The $r = 0.70$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
37	
38	Family-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \citep{kuncheva2003measures}.
39	
40	\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe document a descriptive regime transition in LLM judge panel vulnerability: individual weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) dominates at $K{=}3$; at $K{\\geq}5$, diversity structure ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes the primary predictor. The aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association is attack-mechanism-dependent (targeted positive, shared-bias negative); Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$. Because $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ are structurally correlated ($r{=}0.70$), the independent causal contribution of diversity remains only partially separable even under capability-matched subsampling (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). For deployment: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual robustness; at $K{\\geq}5$, prefer correlated robustness profiles. Causal identification requires training-based diversity control \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}.\n",

        "lines": [
          " \\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}",
          " \\label{app:diversity_capability}",
          " ",
          "-The $r = 0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.",
          "+The $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.",
          " ",
          " Family-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}.",
          " "
        ]

_source line 5095 · 2026-05-23T14:13:14.574Z_

[Director 指令] **[Patch] 紧急修正 — ε 敏感性声明错误（Reviewer 标 CRITICAL）**

appendix.tex 约 L259 的 ε 敏感性段落中 "standardized coefficient differences between ε=10⁻⁶ and ε=10⁻⁹ are <0.01" 是**错误的**。

实际数据：
- K=3: 最大 Δβ_std = 0.102，9/12 条件 >0.01，3 个条件 K_eff 方向翻转
- K=5: 最大 Δβ_std = 0.085，8/12 条件 >0.01，但方向全部一致且全部 p<<0.001

**必须重写该段落**，如实报告。建议改为类似：

"Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to ε at K=3 (maximum Δβ_std = 0.10 between ε=10⁻⁶ and ε=10⁻⁹, with three conditions exhibiting K_eff sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At K=5, all K_eff coefficient directions and significance levels are preserved across ε values (maximum Δβ_std = 0.085, all p < 0.001), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (§4.6), which avoids the log+ε transformation entirely, provides 93.1\% directional concordance with the main OLS results."

同时删除任何其他位置出现的 "<0.01" 或 "coefficient estimates are stable" 类似声明（如果有的话），确保全文不存在与此矛盾的表述。

编译后确认页数。

```text
255	
256	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
257	\label{app:epsilon}
258	
259	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Despite this variation, standardized coefficient differences between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$ are $<$0.01, and qualitative conclusions (significance patterns, directional signs) are identical. FDR significance counts are stable across all three $\epsilon$ values. As an $\epsilon$-free robustness check, fractional logit regression \citep{papke1996econometric} achieves 93.1\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the regime transition pattern (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
260	
261	\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}
262	\label{app:power_analysis}
263	
264	Table~\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an informative null (true effect $<$0.14 SD); conversely, the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.
```

```text
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our study has three principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence; we address this with wild cluster bootstrap ($K$-position max-$p$), but the correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, partly explaining the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 ($K{=}5$) to 5/12 ($K{=}7$). Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface differs structurally from open-ended evaluation; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization. Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; the transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified, and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
41	
42	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/appendix.tex",
  "old_string": "The log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Despite this variation, standardized coefficient differences between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$ are $<$0.01, and qualitative conclusions (significance patterns, directional signs) are identical. FDR significance counts are stable across all three $\\epsilon$ values. As an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check, fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} achieves 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the regime transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).",
  "new_string": "The log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\\Delta\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions and significance levels are preserved across $\\epsilon$ values (maximum $\\Delta\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.085$, all $p < 0.001$), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 93.1\\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks})."
}

    "oldString": "The log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Despite this variation, standardized coefficient differences between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$ are $<$0.01, and qualitative conclusions (significance patterns, directional signs) are identical. FDR significance counts are stable across all three $\\epsilon$ values. As an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check, fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} achieves 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the regime transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).",
    "newString": "The log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\\Delta\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions and significance levels are preserved across $\\epsilon$ values (maximum $\\Delta\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.085$, all $p < 0.001$), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 93.1\\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).",

[Director 指令] [Patch] 两项附录数据整合 + 最终检查。引用不需要验证。投稿目标 EMNLP 2026 Long Paper，8 页限制。

**任务 1: Temperature Pilot 附录段落（新增）**

在 appendix.tex 的 Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis 之后（§Power Analysis 之前）新增一个简短 section：

```
\section{Temperature Sensitivity Pilot}
\label{app:temperature}
```

数据来源：`docs/paper/data/temperature_pilot_summary.json`

内容要点（2-3 句话即可）：
- 用 3 个模型 (gpt-4.1-nano, gemini-3.5-flash, claude-3-haiku) 在 t∈{0.0, 0.3, 0.7, 1.0} 四个温度下评估 K_eff 稳定性
- K_eff 最大变动 Δ < 0.034（MMLU 0.033, ARC 0.027）
- 结论：decoding randomness 不显著影响 K_eff，clean MI 计算对温度变化稳健

**任务 2: K_eff 分布统计附录段落（新增）**

在 Temperature Sensitivity Pilot 之后新增简短 section：

```
\section{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Distribution Statistics}
\label{app:keff_dist}
```

数据来源：`docs/paper/data/keff_distribution_corrected.json`

内容要点：
- K=3: mean=2.18, SD=0.30, range [1.47, 2.80], n=455
- K=5: mean=3.35, SD=0.42, range [2.16, 4.30], n=3003
- K=7: mean=4.53, SD=0.47, range [2.97, 5.55], n=6435
- 一句话评论：K_eff 在各 K 值下有充足变异，均值随 K 线性增长但低于理论上限 K（反映模型间非零相关性）

**任务 3: 最终一致性检查**

完成任务 1-2 后，快速扫一遍主文本和附录，确认没有遗留的 placeholder、TODO、或前后数字不一致。特别关注：
- appendix ε sensitivity 段落中 K=5 的 "maximum Δβ_std = 0.085" 是否与 epsilon_sensitivity_k5.json 一致
- 所有 r 值的 K 标注是否正确（K=3 时 r=0.70, K=5 时 r=0.81）
- Pool A 模型数 = 9（不是 6）

写完后编译检查页数。主文本 ≤ 8 页是硬约束。

```text
256	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
257	\label{app:epsilon}
258	
259	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions and significance levels are preserved across $\epsilon$ values (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.085$, all $p < 0.001$), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 93.1\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
260	
261	\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}
262	\label{app:power_analysis}
263	
264	Table~\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an informative null (true effect $<$0.14 SD); conversely, the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.
265	
266	\begin{table}[h]
267	\centering
```

34	
35	$\eta_{\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation, ARC $\times$ score manipulation, and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 3 $\eta_{\max}$-dominant, 6 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 1 condition where neither predictor reaches significance. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.
36	
```

    "snippet": "1\t\\appendix\n2\t\n3\t\\section{Proof of Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}}\n4\t\\label{app:proof}\n5\t\n6\tThe replaced judge $J_K'$ is \\emph{pivotal} exactly when $t{-}1$ of the remaining $K{-}1$ judges have already failed. Formally, let $S = \\sum_{j=1}^{K-1} X_j^a$ denote the number of failures among the shared judges. The panel fails if and only if the $K$-th judge also fails when $S = t{-}1$, or when $S \\geq t$ regardless of the $K$-th judge:\n7\t\\begin{align}\n8\t\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) &= \\Pr(S \\geq t) + \\Pr(S = t{-}1) \\cdot \\Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \\mid S = t{-}1).\n9\t\\end{align}\n10\tSince $\\Pr(X_{K'}^a {=} 1 \\mid \\mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \\mathbf{x}_{-K}) \\geq \\Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \\mid \\mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \\mathbf{x}_{-K})$ for all attacks $a$ and realizations $\\mathbf{x}_{-K}$ (by hypothesis), the second term is weakly larger for $\\mathcal{P}'$ than for $\\mathcal{P}$ under every attack. Taking the supremum over attacks preserves the inequality: $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}') \\geq \\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$. The result holds under arbitrary inter-judge correlations and any attack space $\\mathcal{A}$.\n11\t\n12\t\\section{Robustness Correction Methods}\n13\t\\label{app:robustness_methods}\n14\t\n15\tThe 455 panels are constructed combinatorially from 15 shared judges: each judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ panels, inducing correlated residuals across panel-level observations. Standard OLS assumes independent errors, potentially inflating significance. We apply three corrections.\n16\t\n17\t\\paragraph{Clustered Standard Errors.} Because each panel of $K{=}3$ judges does not map to a single cluster, we perform three separate one-way clusterings (one for each judge position in the ordered panel tuple) using CR1 bias-corrected sandwich estimators (13 clusters per position). The reported standard error for each coefficient is the maximum across the three clusterings, yielding the most conservative inference.\n18\t\n19\t\\paragraph{Mixed Effects.} For each of the three judge positions, we fit a linear mixed-effects model with a random intercept for the judge occupying that position, estimating $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ as fixed effects. The reported $p$-value for each coefficient is the maximum across the three models.\n20\t\n21\t\\paragraph{Wild Cluster Bootstrap.} We perform $B{=}9{,}999$ bootstrap resamples using Rademacher weights, clustering by each of the three panel positions in turn and taking the maximum $p$-value across positions (the most conservative aggregation). This approach avoids distributional assumptions on the cluster structure.\n22\t\n23\t\\section{Extended Analysis}\n24\t\\label{app:extended_analysis}\n25\t\n26\t\\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}\n27\t\\label{app:bft_reversal}\n28\t\n29\tUnder standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\\eta_{\\max}$ significant in 9 of 12 conditions and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in 7. Non-independence correction reveals an asymmetry: $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 5 to 8 of 12 conditions depending on correction method (clustered SE: 8/12; mixed effects: 7/12; wild cluster bootstrap: 5/12), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses to 0--1/12 (clustered SE 0/12; bootstrap 1/12; mixed effects 3/12). The directional pattern (point estimates predominantly positive) persists, but statistical confidence is limited. The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship that becomes artifactual under adaptive attack (0--1/12 after correction).\n30\t\n31\tUnder OLS, the sole negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient is ARC $\\times$ sycophancy ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$, FDR $p = 0.013$), though this effect does not survive non-independence correction (clustered $p = 0.441$). The GAM taxonomy subsumes this individual exception: the directional pattern across all 6 shared-bias conditions is the stronger evidence, not any single condition's significance.\n32\t\n33\t\\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}\n34\t\\label{app:diversity_capability}\n35\t\n36\tThe $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.\n37\t\n38\tFamily-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}.\n39\t\n40\t\\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}\n41\t\\label{app:aggregation_details}\n42\t\n43\tBayesian calibration is associated with a shift in the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient from predominantly positive under majority vote to predominantly negative (6 of 7 significant coefficients negative). The mechanism is calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, effectively implementing robustness screening within the aggregation rule itself. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors almost perfectly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.89$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \\citep{tambe2011security}.\n44\t\n45\t\\begin{figure}[h]\n46\t\\centering\n47\t\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}\n48\t\\caption{Distribution of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients across aggregation methods. Bayesian aggregation is associated with a sign change in $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (1 positive, 6 negative), contrasting sharply with majority voting (7 positive, 1 negative).}\n49\t\\label{fig:aggregation}\n50\t\\end{figure}\n51\t\n52\t\\subsection{Aggregation at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}5$}{K=5}}\n53\t\\label{app:aggregation_k5}\n54\t\n55\tTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5} extends the aggregation analysis to $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels) under wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$. Bayesian calibration neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12, $\\bar{R}^2{=}0.081$), contrasting with majority vote where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (7/12). Threshold voting retains high predictability ($\\bar{R}^2{=}0.506$) with both predictors significant (6/12 and 10/12). The Bayesian neutralization at $K{=}5$ extends the reversal observed at $K{=}3$ (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}): calibrated confusion matrices effectively remove both individual weakness and structural diversity as predictive channels.\n56\t\n57\t\\begin{table}[h]\n58\t\\centering\n59\t\\small\n60\t\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n61\t\\toprule\n62\t\\textbf{Aggregation} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{\\bar{R}^2}$ \\\\\n63\t\\midrule\n64\tMajority   & 0/12  & 7/12  & 0.256 \\\\\n65\tWeighted   & 1/12  & 9/12  & 0.249 \\\\\n66\tThreshold  & 6/12  & 10/12 & 0.506 \\\\\n67\tBayesian   & 1/12  & 0/12  & 0.081 \\\\\n68\t\\bottomrule\n69\t\\end{tabular}\n70\t\\caption{Aggregation method comparison at $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels, wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Bayesian calibration neutralizes both predictors, while majority vote preserves the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regime transition. Compare Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation} ($K{=}3$).}\n71\t\\label{tab:aggregation_k5}\n72\t\\end{table}\n73\t\n\n... [261 lines truncated] ..."

256	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
257	\label{app:epsilon}
258	
259	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions and significance levels are preserved across $\epsilon$ values (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.085$, all $p < 0.001$), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 93.1\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
260	
261	\section{Temperature Sensitivity Pilot}
